NEWSLETTER · 28 Oct 2026, 21:46

A transatlantic love triangle

Global power is in flux. Your daily guide to what comes next.

By JACK BLANCHARD

Send tips here | View in your browser

POLITICO Named Digiday’s Most Innovative Publisher

Yesterday POLITICO was named 2026 Most Innovative Publisher by Digiday. We are proud of this honor and of the incredible efforts of our team around the world. We also know the credit starts with you, our readers and subscribers.

Everything we have built this year, including our expansions to Australia and Spain, Decoded by POLITICO, the POLITICO Pub and our mobile app, began with the same question: What do the people who count on us need next? Your feedback is how we find the answer, and your trust is what we work to earn every day.

Thank you. We are not finished. There is more coming, and we will keep building it for you.

— POLITICO CEO Goli Sheikholeslami and Global Editor-in-Chief Jonathan Greenberger

Welcome to POLITICO Forecast. This is Jack Blanchard, currently reading this just-published think-piece by POLITICO’s Alex Burns on the lessons Donald Trump can learn from the elections in Israel and Brazil. Drop me a line.

5 THINGS THAT MATTERED TODAY

— The U.S. Federal Reserve held interest rates steady, six days out from the midterm election.

— Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu claimed yesterday’s deadlocked election was “stolen by Arab parties,” and demanded a police inquiry.

— Scores of people died in a high-speed rail crash in Spain, less than one month from the country’s general election.

— Defeated presidential candidate Flávio Bolsonaro and his family landed in Florida after fleeing Brazil.

— OpenAI is launching an election “war room” to identify any interference efforts in the U.S. midterm elections, POLITICO scooped.

THE NEXT NEWS CYCLE

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney delivers a speech to European Parliament in Strasbourg, eastern France, on Sept. 17, 2026. | Jean-Christophe Verhaegen/AFP via Getty Images

FRENCH(-CANADIAN) KISS: A transatlantic love affair which swept from the snowy peaks of the Swiss Alps down to the medieval heart of the Rhine Valley will glide into Montreal tomorrow, as EU leaders seek to consummate their relationship with Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney.

The euro elite fell for Carney hard in Davos in January, after he delivered his now-famous “middle powers” rallying cry on preparing for a world beyond the American security blanket.

A first date in Strasbourg last month went well, with EU Commission President Ursula von der Leyen using her annual State of the Union speech — with Carney present as guest of honor — to open the door to Canada’s “associate membership” of the union. Carney was a little stand-offish, but returned the favor with a warm speech to the EU Parliament the following day.

Tomorrow things get more serious. At a two-day EU-Canadian summit in Montreal, Carney and von der Leyen will seek to hammer out the first steps of a formal relationship. Items on the agenda include closer trade ties, shared defense and energy projects, and enhanced Canadian membership in Europe’s prestigious student exchange program, Erasmus.

The problem, of course — as the late Princess Diana might have put it — is that there are three people in this marriage. And watching all this from afar will be Donald Trump, leader of Canada’s erstwhile closest partner the United States, who has already made his feelings crystal clear.

“I think it’s laughable,” the U.S. president said last month, when asked about Canadian associate-membership of the EU. Trump threatened to cease all trade with Europe if he deems the new alliance “a hostile act.”

On the first part, he needn’t worry. EU capitals, too, have balked at von der Leyen’s ill-defined “associate-member” tag — a status that does not actually exist within Europe’s single-tier structure. The phrase has already been dropped from a draft agreement prepared for the leaders to sign before the summit even gets underway.

On the second, both sides are at pains to insist — in public, at least — that there is nothing “hostile” about closer EU-Canada relations.

“They’re always very keen to say this isn’t against anyone,” POLITICO’s Chief EU Correspondent, Zoya Sheftalovich, tells Forecast in a voice note from Montreal this morning. “The EU wants to show that Canada is one of its closest like-minded countries — that they share values, they share principles, they share their approach to international affairs.

“They’re careful to not make it seem like there’s any sort of anti-Trump sentiment behind it — but I think that is obviously the undercurrent of this whole thing,” Zoya goes on. “The EU and Canada are both being forced to deal with a world that has changed, with norms that have changed, and they’re trying desperately to hold on to a vision of what the world used to be like — while you’ve got the likes of Donald Trump essentially pushing the world in the opposite direction.”

Whether Trump — six days out from the U.S. midterm elections — is paying close enough attention to actually type out an angry Truth Social remains to be seen. But for both sides at tomorrow’s summit, it’s worth the risk.

The EU — still smarting from Brexit and facing the threat of growing anti-EU movements in France, Germany and elsewhere — is desperate to present itself as an attractive friend and partner for wealthy nations like Canada. Carney, meanwhile, has spent the past two years piggybacking on anti-Trump sentiment, and a closer relationship with Europe plays perfectly with his “Canada Strong” message. Tomorrow he’ll use his C-suite credentials to bring together CEOs from some of Canada and Europe’s biggest firms.

“We know from POLITICO polling that a lot of Canadians support deeper and closer ties with Europe over the U.S. now,” POLITICO’s Canada-based trade reporter Zi-Ann Lum tells Forecast. “Of course [Trump’s reaction] is a concern, but I don’t think Carney will be constrained by that possibility. Surprise from Trump is the norm now. Canadian officials expect to be surprised. They’re just bracing for the next thing, and then the next.”

FUTURE INTELLIGENCE: IMPEACHMENTS INCOMING

U.S. Democrats look all but certain to win back the House of Representatives in next week’s midterm elections. And if they do, they’ll be entering Congress with a hit list.

Lawmakers are keen to investigate President Trump, his family and his allies. And first in line could be Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, who’s staring down the threat of impeachment.

Outgoing Republican Rep. Thomas Massie — who’s been locked in a bitter, years-long feud with the president and is now nearing the end of his term — introduced the push to impeach Hegseth last month, accusing the defense secretary of “high crimes and misdemeanors.” (Hegseth’s Hail Mary: a possible exit to the Senate, where he may be vying for appointment to Tennessee Sen. Marsha Blackburn’s soon-to-be open seat).

“We’re going to hold him accountable in the same way that we held Pam Bondi and Kristi Noem accountable,” House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries told MS NOW, referring to two controversial members of Trump’s original Cabinet who were fired after disastrous committee hearings. “Pete Hegseth needs to be pushed aside by the president — or we’re going to have to deal with that situation if he is not.”

With control of the House, Democrats would have the power to convene oversight hearings, issue subpoenas — and launch impeachments.

Plenty of voters, too, are eager to see impeachment back on the menu. Half of Americans support impeaching Trump himself, according to The POLITICO Poll. And more than two-thirds believe that if Democrats flip the House, they’ll try to — again.

But that appetite doesn’t line up with the political reality. The House has already tried Trump twice — once in 2019 and again in 2021. Those efforts did little to harm his political fortunes, and he soon stormed back into the White House. A third impeachment could only end in conviction and Trump’s removal if two-thirds of the Senate signed it off — which looks impossible in the current climate.

Top Democrats fear such an exercise could be political suicide with voters demanding action on the cost of living. The top three House Democrats killed an impeachment attempt last month, saying such a move is “not anything that is top of the agenda for House Democrats.”

Although Trump might be off the table, his Cabinet may still prove fair game. Hegseth is, of course, the first politician in the line of fire. House Democrats have also introduced measures to impeach Education Secretary Linda McMahon and Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. Democratic midterm voters are eyeing impeachments across the board, even for Vice President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio.

QUOTE OF THE DAY

“Is that it? … The prime minister came in promising to reverse 40 years of neoliberalism. Instead, we got 4p off gherkins.”

—U.K. opposition leader Kemi Badenoch lays into PM Andy Burnham’s newly-released autumn budget, which reduced tariffs on supermarket goods.

HARD POWER: TRAGEDY IN TOLEDO

Emergency services work on the site of a train collision in Toledo, Spain, on Oct. 28, 2026. | Javier Soriano/AFP via Getty Images

Today’s high-speed rail crash in Toledo is a full-scale disaster for Spain. At time of writing, 68 people are confirmed dead, with hundreds more injured. The images are horrific. Scores of Spanish families are in mourning. And this in a country still reeling from the Adamuz rail disaster of Jan. 2026, in which 46 people died.

While politics rightly takes a back seat at moments of national tragedy, the timing for Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez could hardly be worse. Sánchez is less than one month out from a snap general election and already faced a near-impossible task to cling to power. Now the growing national rage at a second deadly rail crash in a single year will be focused, at least in part, upon his government.

“It’s a disaster for Sánchez’s electoral prospects,” POLITICO’s senior editor in Madrid, Angel Villarino, tells Forecast. “He wasn’t going to be able to form a government anyway, but the campaign was going relatively well for him. It was built on optimism and good vibes, and this shatters all of that.”

The campaign fallout has been relatively lowkey so far, especially given the Spanish government immediately announced three days of national mourning. But expect that to change in the days ahead.

“Every party is now adjusting course,” Angel said. “It will be interesting to see how long they wait before dragging it into the political debate. Vox [Spain’s far-right party, currently polling in third place] has already started.” Vox and its closely-affiliated Solidarity union published posters today blaming the crash on “corruption” and stating bluntly that Sánchez is to blame.

Conservative opposition leader Alberto Núñez Feijóo— widely expected to be Spain’s next prime minister, in coalition with Vox — was more restrained, although his Popular Party (PP) posted an image on social media which stated: “Enough is enough.”

“What’s not clear is how this [accident] affects the balance between the PP and Vox,” Angel says. “Votes were already shifting toward Vox, and this will probably accelerate that — though we don’t know for sure.” The better the far-right party performs on Nov. 27, the more clout it is likely to wield in a future conservative-led government.

SOFT POWER: MAO MONEY, MAO PROBLEMS

Staying with Spain for a moment: “We have taken note of the apology and expect them to follow through on their promises,” China’s Ambassador to Madrid, Yao Jing, told my Madrid Playbook colleagues this morning. He was referring to the grovelling apology from Real Madrid soccer star Kylian Mbappé after Chinese brands cancelled a raft of sponsorship deals over a jokey reference to the former Chinese leader Mao Zedong.

And it wasn’t just Mbappé saying sorry — not once China had suspended its ultra-lucrative contract to show La Liga matches, anyway.

Real Madrid quickly issued its own apology. La Liga boss Javier Tebas also said sorry — “we cannot allow this!” — and held crisis talks with Ambassador Yao today. Yao told Madrid Playbook he now expects Spain’s football elite to demonstrate “respect and decency through concrete actions — and to refrain from acting against public sentiment again.”

Mbappé’s crime, to be clear, was to refer to Chairman Mao as a “dictator” — a view widely held by Western historians given he was unelected, wielded absolute power from the center and is generally held responsible for vast numbers of civilian deaths. In China he is portrayed as a revolutionary hero.

Unsurprisingly, the La Liga grovelling has not gone well in the U.S.

“Mao was a dictator,” the U.S. House Select Committee on China said in a statement, “and by orders of magnitude he did more harm to the Chinese people than a comment from a soccer player ever could. Mao was the leader of the CCP for decades and his dictatorship and disastrous policies killed tens of millions of Chinese people … The real disgrace in this news story is the companies rushing to apologize so they can continue to do business in China.” See you tomorrow.

THURSDAY’S AGENDA

— Two-day EU-Canada summit gets underway in Montreal.

— President Trump to hold midterm election rally in South Carolina.

— European Central Bank announcement on interest rates, with the current rate expected to remain unchanged.

— U.N. Security Council debates on AI and maritime security.

— Q3 results from tech giants Amazon, Intel and Samsung.

28 October, 2026

Alex Binley

Hello. Spain is grieving, its prime minister has said in the wake of the country's deadliest rail disaster in more than a decade. With the majority of votes counted in Israel, the country looks to be in a political deadlock. Plus, see Stonehenge artefacts that are going on display in Germany.

TOP OF THE AGENDA

How passengers' rail journey turned to disaster in Spain

A stationary train was 'struck from behind', rail operator Iryo has said. Credit: EPA/Shutterstock

Passengers have described travellers being "thrown into the air" https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpbs2yw4R6Exm7RFagyc0gvd7tK7vhnQQeg1YT0Q52zPTDtuFjCC33v7lTYOG3qNag1XUgm5e2Pibf_sRWAYx6uRjbcoDzQPgAD8Isc when a high-speed train crashed into another about 140km (100 miles) south of Madrid this morning. One woman said she saw bodies under one of the trains and a carriage on fire. At least 68 people were killed and 80 are being treated for injuries, according to authorities. Spain's transport minister said an "inexplicable" decision by the control centre led to the crash after a computer failure initially caused trains to halt. In January another disaster on the same line, but much further south, led to 46 deaths. "This latest crash will intensify the doubts about the rail system's safety and criticism of the government's management of it," Guy Hedgecoe reports from the scene https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpbs2yw50ICwC_yOxFkXB1CGToyCElQzxgIOAEYY84scA6phEjoW28tp47_rSdhuCW6s9WLVYIQUyTM2wozw-02kksveBv0wUVZd14s .What we know https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpbs2yw6msEUM4R7u4ZvQLGgy2auEJ1Tt5sa3zTj2gzsmwBDT92aX1CADP9ffMZi7IEG1mS6FDHXddz3u37_4nthr1NAGhjDQtpXZls

YOUR QUESTIONS ANSWERED

The different scenarios that could play out to break Israel's political deadlock

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpbs2yxM7KBHemZucQxHPqznLalB_p6XKT098L7bi1-sXkkb6lOPPCDP4RI5Cb6FshcipYb0tVrPTmB2c3x0hbigtAMVIBnfwpR7GLk If no majority can be formed, the default outcome would be new elections. Credit: AFP via Getty Images

With more than 85% of election results now counted, Israel appears to be in a political deadlock. Neither incumbent Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, nor his key challenger Gadi Eisenkot look set to have sufficient seats for a majority. Short of a new election, what could happen in order for a new government to be formed?

Tom Bennett, senior digital journalist

Could Netanyahu persuade Eisenkot to join him in a national unity government?

Netanyahu would have to ditch the far-right and ultra-Orthodox parties he is currently in coalition with, while Eisenkot, who framed himself as the only alternative to Netanyahu, would have to abandon his key platform of ousting his political rival from power.

Or could Eisenkot form a coalition with Arab parties?

Eisenkot and every other Jewish party in his bloc bar one have already ruled out entering government with them. Forming an alliance with the Arab parties would be a seismic shift for Israeli politics, but right now it's one of the only routes to forming a majority.

What about right-wingers from the opposition joining a Netanyahu-led majority?

Netanyahu could try to persuade right-wing parties in Eisenkot's bloc to break away. The two most likely candidates are Naftali Bennett, joint-leader of the Beyachad alliance, and Avigdor Lieberman, leader of the Yisrael Beiteinu party, but they both campaigned explicitly to oust him.

Is election inevitable? https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpbs2yxNcbXtxEDrIB8YovBiMS4IW-fr-x5UusxR-9tKb6Rehd0XBGJV_cjIgZRGWXbkeMt8xD1d_b92hTTvoExvkABBnv1WiC7Qxng

TOP OF THE AGENDA

Israel's Arab parties could be kingmakers

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpH-jLPSzqAVlw10eH4Fpc3TSpEZeUXeSN4RCPFZs63EsMTR798_mqqyueRoPqOXcH9R83Pc79vL7EoJ4ZH9Z7rS8I6AvZHV1HBT0wcq A high number of absentee ballots - mostly representing the votes of Israeli soldiers - are yet to be counted. Credit: Reuters

Overnight, both veteran Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and former military chief Gadi Eisenkot pledged to form the next government while addressing their supporters. But with 60% of the votes now counted, neither of their blocs are on course to secure a majority. Netanyahu's Likud party has performed better than expected and his existing right-wing bloc appears to be just shy of a 61-seat majority in the 120-seat parliament. However, this could change as more votes are counted, writes correspondent Yolande Knell. To command a majority, Eisenkot would likely need to join forces with Israel's Arab-led parties, which seem to have equalled or gained on their previous record number of seats, Yolande notes. Yet on the campaign trail, Eisenkot and other Jewish opposition parties ruled out working with them, citing a lack of shared values.

BEYOND THE HEADLINES

The border exposing fragile India-China relations

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpH-jLPrnZVf36FoZTSaTORVQmLJwUps0Axpht9YLZoBKog33dJaHtNItPSDIZ73rkDJvMwNHr7OQ6uGfO-dMHrah2tTVaDgrHYHPRSx Much of the Himalayan frontier remains undemarcated, with the countries making overlapping territorial claims. Credit: AFP via Getty

India and China have seen ups and downs in their ties over decades, especially over the disputed Himalayan frontier, which lies between the two countries. But following deadly military clashes in 2020, India-China relations seemed to be progressing on a cautious path to recovery. Then, a few days ago, India's border police chief said Chinese troops had returned to disputed areas in large numbers earlier this year, pitching tents several kilometres inside territory claimed by India. The remarks have triggered a political controversy in Delhi.

Here's why https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDMsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABpH-jLPsJ2qDpWBEzGAifaQmltiWZLlyLD_qRqBwjwK-MUVSIjdye6jZgFbrN74j8z2snvyO5Wir6zvIYjuQ7TPTTdWAEfFbpLxwtwlv

Snapshot of the day October 28, 2026 Siddharth Varadarajan, Kalrav Joshi, Anirudh S.K. After being denied permission to use several venues, the Congress announced on Wednesday that the INDIA bloc of opposition parties would hold a “public impeachment” of CEC Gyanesh Kumar [ https://substack.com/redirect/17566f29-3f4b-4113-8ea5-3bb01777ceb6?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] at party president Mallikarjun Kharge’s residence on Rajaji Marg, refusing to “succumb” to what it alleges is Delhi Police’s obstruction of its “Vote Chori” rally preparations, as a warm-up event ahead of the rally scheduled for November 1 at the Ramlila Maidan. Speaking to reporters at Ramlila Maidan, Congress MP and general secretary (organisation) K.C. Venugopal said the Ashoka Hotel had denied permission for the October 30 event after the “police came into the picture” – highlighting the Delhi Police’s role in obstructing opposition protests at the behest of Union Home Minister Amit Shah. The Cockroach Jantra Party has also extended its support to the INDIA bloc’s November 1 protest march [ https://substack.com/redirect/66a6a714-b298-4c8a-959a-0ecd7cc18662?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. The BJP president Nitin Nabin led a padayatra through Chandni Chowk in Delhi [ https://substack.com/redirect/e490540c-7120-41b5-a68b-d36b3e1191c4?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] to denounce the Opposition’s campaign. The barricades and stifling police bandobast that have accompanied every other political mobilisation on the streets the past few weeks were conspicuously absent. The Supreme Court has questioned the Election Commission of India (ECI) over its insistence that voters already on the electoral rolls fill out Form 6 to be included in the draft roll under the Special Intensive Revision (SIR), simply because officials could not reach them. Chief Justice of India Surya Kant called the requirement erroneous, pointing out that Form 6 is meant for first-time voters, who must declare they have never been enrolled before. “Instead of burdening them to approach you, why don’t you reach out to them?” he asked. The bench also clarified that it had never adjudicated on or approved the ECI’s use of ‘logical discrepancies’, allowing it only as an interim measure in the West Bengal case, Justice Joymalya Bagchi said. The distinction raises fresh questions about the deletion of 27 lakh voters in the state. Meanwhile, Aparna Bhattacharya lists down five problems in the ECI’s explanation [ https://substack.com/redirect/9a878e0d-62ab-44b1-b1a2-e5f7605b235f?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] for the 16 lakh appeals against Bengal voters, drawing on the poll body’s affidavit, court orders and appeal records. In another significant exchange, the court questioned the ECI over the 47 lakh people on the ASDD (Absentee / Shifted / Duplicate / Deceased) list, asking why they could not be given an opportunity to submit enumeration forms or file Form 8. Read [ https://substack.com/redirect/0cc041e9-66b3-4cb0-89d0-6e12ce919b15?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]LiveLaw [ https://substack.com/redirect/0cc041e9-66b3-4cb0-89d0-6e12ce919b15?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]’s thread on X [ https://substack.com/redirect/0cc041e9-66b3-4cb0-89d0-6e12ce919b15?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] for more from today’s proceedings. Meanwhile, despite the 30-day statutory deadline having passed, the ECI has yet to respond to an RTI query by activist Commodore (Retd) Lokesh Batra [ https://substack.com/redirect/aa80a1e9-5c17-4dab-9f69-2cada686f446?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] seeking details and copies of the recorded dissent and disagreements of Election Commissioners Vivek Joshi and Sukhbir Singh Sandhu to the conduct of the Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of electoral rolls 14 times in 10 months, as reported by the Express. Amidst nation-wide calls for CEC ...

Supporting Student Mental Health Takes a Toll on Teachers

Policy Currents | The newsletter for policy people Web version: https://www.rand.org/policy-currents/2026/10/28.html

** Oct. 28, 2026 ------------------------------------------------------------ Supporting Student Mental Health Takes a Toll on Teachers

Schools often lack enough counselors and other mental health professionals, and teachers have stepped into the gap. A new RAND study surveyed more than 800 U.S. public school teachers about how their efforts to support student well-being affect them.

Here's what we found:

- Nearly all teachers said they provided at least one type of student support during the 2025-26 school year. Supports include checking in on students, collaborating with counselors or social workers, and helping students navigate personal challenges.

- Teachers spent an average of about eight hours per week providing student supports. That's 17 percent of their total working hours.

- Teachers who frequently provided more mental health support to students were more likely to say they had high emotional costs from teaching: 62 percent compared with 38 percent of teachers who did not frequently provide any supports.

- Teachers who said providing support had recently become harder were more likely to report indicators of poor well-being, including frequent job-related stress, difficulty coping with stress, symptoms of depression, and burnout.

It's clear that, for many teachers, supporting students' mental health and well-being is taking a toll. Schools could help ease the burden by connecting students and families with counselors, social workers, and community services.

Read more: https://www.rand.org/pubs/research_reports/RRA4404-6.html

The Least Bad Option in Iran

The U.S. war with Iran began exactly eight months ago today. What should the United States do now? In Foreign Policy, RAND's Raphael Cohen and Howard Shatz weigh three options: ramp the war back up, walk away, or stay the course. They argue that staying the course is strategically prudent. The U.S. blockade and wider economic pressure campaign are moving in the direction Washington wants: Iran's economy is imploding, and further stress could push Tehran to the negotiating table. The main downside is that this approach won't bring a quick end to the conflict. But "sometimes an imperfect option is actually the right one," Cohen and Shatz write, "if only because the others are that much worse."

Read more: https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/26/iran-war-trump-options-escalation-blockade-hormuz/

Biosecurity in the AI Era

AI could speed up cancer research. It could also help bad actors create bioweapons. On a new episode of the Policy Minded podcast, RAND's Steph Guerra explains how to reduce those risks while preserving legitimate scientific progress. Guerra emphasizes that, although it's hard to know whether an AI model could be used in a biological attack, that's no reason to wait on building defenses. "If we wait too long until these capabilities are demonstrably effective," she says, "it will be too late."

Listen now: https://www.rand.org/pubs/podcasts/policy-minded/2026/biosecurity-in-the-ai-era.html

The Case El-Sayed Can Make to Black Voters

View this post on the web at https://www.thebulwark.com/p/the-case-abdul-el-sayed-can-make-to-black-michigan-voters

A NEW ADVERTISEMENT running against Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan’s Senate race targets black voters by arguing the Democratic nominee takes them for granted. “Abdul El-Sayed’s platform is nearly five thousand words,” says a narrator [ https://substack.com/redirect/8e9c966d-bea7-41c4-a7d0-851407f73561?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. “He names fifteen foreign countries he cares about. A single mention of black people? Not once.” The narrator then lists issues like housing and school choice as important to black voters on which El-Sayed allegedly says nothing. “He can’t find the words to talk about us,” the narrator concludes, “and he wonders why we don’t trust him.” The ad’s central premise is an abject lie. If you type “El-Sayed agenda black Americans” into Google, literally the very first hit is a page from his campaign website [ https://substack.com/redirect/e74715c0-caff-4ad7-ad8d-60d68d3b073c?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] called “Abdul El-Sayed Announces Policy Agenda to Empower Michigan’s Black Communities.” The page summarizes (and links to a longer version [ https://substack.com/redirect/2ae4e3e5-65f4-4d2f-a768-d4d1867dc9c3?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] of) what El-Sayed has called his “THRIVE” agenda, released in September. It includes a twenty-one-point plan to help black Michiganders through a variety of programs, including housing assistance and education reform. The attack ad, which comes from an independent political action committee [ https://substack.com/redirect/d2be22ab-cc53-463b-9005-f9c5ff29e2da?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] that opposes El-Sayed, isn’t the only dishonest one [ https://substack.com/redirect/61802042-535c-4138-b691-352cee06beb2?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] circulating in the tightly contested Michigan Senate race. But the ad has extra political significance because of its target audience. To win in Michigan, Democrats have in modern times needed both overwhelming support of black voters and high turnout, too. El-Sayed struggled with these voters during the primary, and his Republican opponent, former congressman Mike Rogers, has been trying to exploit that weakness by appealing directly for their support. The contest for Michigan’s black voters has gotten a ton [ https://substack.com/redirect/54706201-8e03-4d16-9c40-45666e7c623b?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] of [ https://substack.com/redirect/687dccbe-966c-4bde-9331-eb2584166ac0?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] media [ https://substack.com/redirect/e76c06c9-4a78-4357-a3e4-5b8fba5fcc6e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] attention [ https://substack.com/redirect/2645cebc-b26c-42b6-a9bd-266ec4c09719?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] (including from me! [ https://substack.com/redirect/83df7ffc-f0d7-43ec-aa20-06d4fa8adfc5?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]). It’s a genuinely important story that sheds light on larger, deeper issues, like historical tensions between black and Arab communities in Michigan and the way black voters think about progressive versus moderate politics in the Democratic party. But something important is missing from the current dialogue: What the two candidates ...

NEWSLETTER · 28 Oct 2026, 14:34

How Do You Solve a Problem Like Elon Musk?

View this post on the web at https://www.thebulwark.com/p/how-do-you-solve-a-problem-like-elon-musk-talarico-texas-trans-money-senate

A new New York Times poll today has some surprising findings in close Senate races: South Carolina is basically a tie at 48 percent for Sen. Darline Graham and the same for Annie Andrews. Florida isn’t nearly as close: Sen. Ashley Moody, at 50 percent, is six points ahead of Democrat Angie Nixon. In Minnesota, Peggy Flanagan is at 54 percent, while Michele Tafoya is basically on the sidelines of her own race. Happy Wednesday. Talarico vs. the Tycoon by Andrew Egger Democrats’ main hope for next week’s elections is to win enough seats to put a check on Donald Trump. But in Texas, James Talarico is trying to create a Democratic template for another problem: How to deal with Elon Musk. There is no preexisting playbook for dealing with Musk, because Musk is something new in American political life. It’s not just that he’s the richest man in the world and the biggest individual donor in American history. (Musk’s political expenditures since 2024 have already surpassed [ https://substack.com/redirect/99dc014e-314e-471f-b252-2f734d5014ee?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] George Soros’s campaign contributions over his entire lifetime.) It’s also his extraordinary level of engagement. If Musk decides he wants a Democrat gone, he doesn’t just throw a bunch of money behind that Democrat’s opponent—he makes it his personal mission to stamp him out. This cycle, Musk has trained his sights on Talarico. Of the astonishing $446 million he has plowed into the midterms, around $180 billion has gone to the Texas Senate race—financial life support that Talarico’s opponent Ken Paxton has desperately needed. Most billionaire political donors would be content to throw their lucre into a PAC to buy up a bunch of ad time and call it a day. Musk goes further. Some of the dirtiest tricks of the cycle seem to be connected to his spending. Speaking of spending: This newsletter is free. Every day. Pretty good deal, right? Sign up so you don’t miss an issue. Last week, I wrote about [ https://substack.com/redirect/120bbdef-0c23-47d7-99a8-8f159404f1eb?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] a Wyoming group calling itself the Texas Transgender Student Union (TTSU) and its apparent attempt to gin up a new trans panic by impersonating a genuine Texas trans-rights group, the Transgender Education Network of Texas (TENT), and sending inflammatory mailers to Texas Republican voters. TENT sued, and yesterday, Texas reporter Steven Monacelli reported [ https://substack.com/redirect/0d237eb4-56b9-4f50-a297-d843929892bf?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] that TTSU had retained an interesting lawyer: Chris Hilton, who defended Ken Paxton during his impeachment and represents Musk in a defamation lawsuit against the organization Media Matters. The trillionaire has other megaphones. On X, where he has 240 million followers and owns the algorithms that ensure everyone sees his posts, he has tweeted, retweeted, or replied to tweets attacking Talarico more than forty times in the last 20 days. That number doesn’t include all the attacks Musk’s shared without mentioning the candidate. Talarico is [ https://substack.com/redirect/f04a0b5f-1192-492a-a49d-fa560318b087?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] “a far left extremist.” Talarico [ https://substack.com/redirect/f04a0b5f-1192-492a-a49d-fa560318b087?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] “hates Texas and America.” There is something “deeply wrong” [ https://substack.com/redirect/16d458e3-63ad-4286-b56f-086115b04c81?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] with “the creep” [ https://substack.com/redirect/16d458e3-63ad-4286-b56f-086115b04c81?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] Talarico who “supports voting fraud” [ https://substack.com/redirect/8b9ed218-1e44-4045-ab7d-47b8663e8e4e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] and “wants to trans your kids and destroy the border.” [ https://substack.com/redirect/7b48a1e7-2bdd-4ef7-8cd9-fd922382b90e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] This last is a recurring theme: Musk has repeatedly claimed that Talarico “will vote to take away your children and transition them.” [ https://substack.com/redirect/b0b20c8c-c244-41c6-83ad-67d74f26b9ee?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] Democrats have wealthy donors of their own, of course. But there’s nothing they can do to match Musk in raw wealth and raw rage. Democrats have only one hope: to poison his donations by turning his involvement in their races into a major liability for the Republicans he supports. That’s exactly what Talarico has been trying to do lately. In his stump speeches and campaign ads over the last week, the Texas Democrat has gone hard after Musk’s enormous investments in Texas, casting him as a shadowy figure single-handedly propping up Paxton’s campaign for his own ends. “In all likelihood, if you’ve seen an attack ad on your screen, that was Elon. If you’ve gotten lies in your mailbox, that was Elon. If you’ve received a text message saying scary things about me, that was Elon too,” Talarico has been saying on the stump [ https://substack.com/redirect/e9ea6691-2e9c-45af-9340-6efb6ca3a88f?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. “Elon Musk wants our land. He wants our water. He wants our taxpayer dollars. He wants his very own personal United States senator. He is trying to buy Texas. But on Nov. 3, we are going to send a very clear message to the world’s first trillionaire: Texas is not for sale.” These sorts of messages can resonate. Think of the success Republicans have had railing against Soros over the years, or the success many Democrats found in primaries just months ago castigating donations from AIPAC. Nobody really likes to think about the way money shapes our politics—and Musk, the hard-right tech mogul who has tied himself so tightly to MAGA, is a more contemptible target than most. Last year, his heavy spending in a Wisconsin Supreme Court contest turned the backwater race into a referendum on Musk himself—a referendum he lost [ https://substack.com/redirect/00d25eee-4a3c-440d-9f56-87908a59b098?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] when the race’s liberal judge won. Talarico had better hope he can do the same thing again. If he can’t—if Musk’s heavy investment in Texas works—Democrats are looking down a long, grim road of similar interventions in the future. The world’s first trillionaire could drop another half-billion for Republicans on every national election for the rest of his life without breaking a sweat. Back to the Future by William Kristol In yesterday’s elections in Israel, Benjamin Netanyahu’s governing coalition and the opposition bloc both seem to have failed to win a clear governing majority. So there will be complicated and confusing maneuvering over the coming weeks and probably months, and it’s unclear who will end up as the next prime minister. What we know is that Israel is pretty evenly divided between (to oversimplify) a nationalist, religious, and authoritarian-inclined coalition on one side, and a liberal, secular, and democratically-inclined coalition on the other. We are familiar with this division: Red America and Blue America. And we’re also familiar with the fact that the two “parties,” to use the term broadly, are pretty evenly balanced. That seems to be the case around the world. One side, one party, prevails—but then loses. The struggle is not resolved once and for all. Here’s the lesson I take from this: We are not getting beyond nationalism, or nativism, or populism, or authoritarianism, any time soon. We can defeat these forces at times, maybe even most of the time and in most places—and it’s very important to do so! But such impulses, such beliefs, such movements are not going away. Neither, as events this year in Hungary and Serbia remind us, are the forces of democratic liberalism. So “normalcy” is no longer what it was for much of the last half of the twentieth century and the early years of the twenty-first: a party system, consisting of two or more mainstream parties, basically committed to a system of liberal democracy, with extremists on the fringe but rarely able to acquire real power. We were fortunate, in many parts of the world, to enjoy such a happy situation in recent decades. But there’s no evidence we’re getting back to that anytime soon. What we seem to be getting back to is the nineteenth century. Then politics tended to be centered on the fight between liberals (broadly speaking), and illiberals or anti-liberals. That struggle, and the various victories and defeats of each side, defined the history of many nations throughout that era. We thought for a while that we’d moved beyond that. We were wrong. It’s important in such a circumstance 1) for the liberals to win more often than they lose, and 2) for liberals to succeed in establishing and strengthening alternate power centers within the government and in the broader society so that one authoritarian victory isn’t the end of the story, but allows for a subsequent liberal victory. One could say much the same thing about international affairs. It seemed for a while that a “rules-based international order” had replaced power politics. But to the degree that was even true, it was temporary. It now turns out that the two views of the world—the liberal and the predatory, to oversimplify again—are in a contest for supremacy, and that neither party clearly has the upper hand. The bad news is that the Putins of the world aren’t going away. Nor is Putinism. The good news is that the Zelenskys of the world are fighting back. So we can no longer assume, in 2026, that liberal democracy is the norm, and that occasional authoritarian threats can be disposed of quickly or with finality. We are engaged in an ongoing contest, and one that seems pretty evenly matched. A couple of days ago I quoted James Madison on “the great struggle of the Epoch between liberty and despotism.” It was the struggle of Madison’s epoch. It will be the struggle of ours, as well. QUICK HITS FLÁVIO FLIES THE COOP: A few days ago, Brazil’s Flávio Bolsonaro, son of former president and convicted coupster Jair Bolsonaro, seemed on the brink of taking power himself. Yesterday, on the heels of his shock loss to incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, Bolsonaro fled the country. The former candidate was not supposed to leave. Although he had not been charged with a crime, Brazilian police had asked him to surrender his passports amid a money-laundering investigation into one of his associates. Instead, Bolsonaro split—crossing the border into Argentina, then boarding a plane bound for the United States. Typically, the Trump administration has not been crazy about Latino men showing up unexpectedly at the U.S. border. But the State Department told [ https://substack.com/redirect/c72421b6-7f5f-4aee-8813-c6b85a29468e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] reporters that it would issue visas to Bolsonaro and his family. It’s nice to have a guy on the inside. ANOTHER ENEMY ESCAPES: Donald Trump has suffered a lot of defeats in his first two years back in office, but none more stinging than his near-total failure to bring successful criminal charges against the enemies he’s desperate to punish. Still, he seems determined to keep trying, no matter how slim the odds of success. Yesterday brought another clunker: A federal grand jury in D.C. declined to sign off on an indictment against Cassidy Hutchinson, a former White House aide and key witness to the January 6th Committee. Here’s the [ https://substack.com/redirect/d7b8dc8f-59dc-46e2-ba7a-f12170f61caa?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]New York Times [ https://substack.com/redirect/d7b8dc8f-59dc-46e2-ba7a-f12170f61caa?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]: Some Justice Department prosecutors had been deeply skeptical that there was a case to be made against Ms. Hutchinson for lying to Congress. They had concluded that it would be difficult to win a conviction against her. But in an unusual arrangement, the investigation was directed to the department’s civil rights division, which typically focuses on issues like police violence and election integrity. The civil rights division is led by Harmeet Dhillon, a Trump loyalist who has effectively reoriented the unit to align with Mr. Trump’s agenda, including by extracting agreements from elite universities that were favorable to the administration. It was once rare for grand jurors to rebuff prosecutors and turn down a request for an indictment, given that the Justice Department has traditionally sought criminal charges only in cases it expects to win. But such failures have become more common for the department under Mr. Trump, who has pushed prosecutors to seek indictments even in cases in which evidence is lacking. As always: Utterly insane that Trump is this hell-bent on punishing former junior staffers who “betrayed” him; extremely satisfying that he continues to fail. Read the whole thing. October 28, 2026 thediplomat.com

Welcome to the latest issue of Diplomat Brief. This week we take a look at what the U.S. midterm elections may mean for Australia. We also examine Timor-Leste’s first year in ASEAN, China’s broken economy, and the South Korean ruling party’s sudden fall from grace.

STORY OF THE WEEK

FEATURE

The 2026 US Midterm Elections: Ramifications for Australia

On November 3, Americans will go to the polls. The 2026 midterm elections in the United States represent a consequential juncture in the evolution of the U.S.-led international order. Their significance lies not merely in the redistribution of legislative power in Washington, but in what the electoral outcome may reveal about the durability of President Donald Trump’s political mandate, the institutional constraints on executive authority, and the future trajectory of U.S. engagement with its allies.

For Australia, the implications are particularly profound.

Canberra’s post-war strategic posture has rested on the assumption that U.S. military predominance, institutional continuity, and alliance commitments will provide an enduring foundation for national security. However, as the last two years reveal, the contemporary United States presents a more complex proposition.

Find out more

VIDEO

Why China Can’t Fix Its Broken Economy

Our YouTube channel, The Diplomat explains major topics and trends in the Asia-Pacific in video form. Our latest video dives deep into a serious problem: China's economy is fundamentally broken. Chinese people just aren’t spending enough money and this is not just a problem for China, it’s a problem for the world.

Milena Maria da Costa Rangel on Timor-Leste’s 1st Year in ASEAN

Timor-Leste joined ASEAN on October 26, 2025. In a new Diplomat interview series hosted by Khoo Ying Hooi, Timor-Leste’s Vice Minister for ASEAN Affairs Milena Maria da Costa Rangel discusses the country’s first year in ASEAN and the political and practical choices confronting Dili.

“We remain grateful for the welcome and solidarity extended to Timor-Leste by ASEAN member states. But becoming a member is only the beginning of a much longer process,” Rangel said.

This Week in Asia

NORTHEAST ASIA

South Korea's Ruling Party Falls From Grace

Since Yoon Suk-yeol's shocking declaration of martial law – and subsequent impeachment – the Democratic Party has had a leg up on Yoon’s People Power Party in polls. That led to two landslide election victories, handing the DP the presidency in 2025 and 12 out of 16 top offices in provinces and metropolitan cities in this year’s local elections. But just four months later, the DP has undergone a stunning fall from grace. It’s now less popular than the PPP. What happened? A series of gaffes and public in-fighting, coupled with controversial implementation of top agenda items like lowering housing prices and prosecutorial reform. High-profile spats with the United States haven’t helped boost confidence in the Lee Jae-myung administration, either. We’re seeing a stunning turnaround in South Korean politics. Now the question is whether the PPP can actually capitalize on it.

Find out more

SOUTH ASIA

Durga Puja in India and Bangladesh: A Tale of 2 Festivals

Durga Puja is a major Hindu festival honoring the ten-armed goddess Durga, who slayed a demon king. It is the biggest festival for Bengalis and for the Bengali diaspora. In the eastern Indian state of West Bengal, this year’s celebrations were the first held under the Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP), which came to power in Bengal just five months ago, after defeating the regional Trinamool Congress (TMC). The celebrations were marred by a stampede in north Kolkata and the bulldozing of bookstalls near puja pandal in south Kolkata. Meanwhile, in Bangladesh – where the Bangladesh Nationalist Party (BNP) has returned to power and with it concerns about the safety of the country’s non-Muslim religious communities are in the air – celebrations went smoothly.

Find out more

SOUTHEAST ASIA

Aung San Suu Kyi Speaks With Her Son

The son of Myanmar’s detained former leader Aung San Suu Kyi this week spoke to his mother for the first time since the military coup of 2021, reiterating calls for her immediate release. In a statement, Kim Aris thanked all of those who had campaigned for his mother’s freedom, but said that his mother “must never be held hostage or used as a bargaining chip.” The call came three days after the military-backed administration released two photos of the Nobel laureate receiving two books and a sealed diplomatic pouch from Britain’s King Charles III. Despite spending most of the five years since the coup in seclusion, with virtually no contact with the outside world, Myanmar’s military-backed government has in recent months, loosened the terms of Aung San Suu Kyi’s detention, in support of its push for diplomatic normalization with key partners including the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN).

Find out more

CENTRAL ASIA

Kazakhstan’s Kurultai Is Muzzling the Press

Journalists ask questions. That’s the job. But in Kazakhstan’s new unicameral parliament, the Kurultai, accredited journalists are no longer allowed to speak to deputies or officials in the hallways. They’re only permitted to ask questions during official briefings, or pre-arranged meetings. And they can only ask on-topic questions, a determination made by the Kurultai. Violating the new rules risks their accreditation. The Kurultai’s press corps have appealed to the Kurultai leadership to change the rules. Journalists, they argued, need to be free to question ministers, deputies, and other officials – who, the appeal pointed out, “rarely appear in public and for whom the public has many questions.”

Find out more

Will There Be a Putin-Trump-Xi Summit?

In the latest episode of the Asia Geopolitics podcast, host Ankit Panda and Katie Putz discuss U.S. President Donald Trump’s upcoming travel itinerary to Asia, to include the ASEAN Summit in Manila, the Philippines, and the APEC Summit in Shenzhen, China. What’s grabbed headlines, however, is the possibility of a trilateral summit with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin. Putin and Trump have commented on the potential summit, highlighting very different agendas, and China has stayed mostly quiet about it.

NEWSLETTER · 28 Oct 2026, 10:55

Democracy Wins in Brazil and Serbia

View this post on the web at https://crookedmedia.substack.com/p/democracy-wins-in-brazil-and-serbia

Tommy and Ben break down Brazilian President Lula da Silva’s stunning runoff victory over Flávio Bolsonaro, including a 10-million-person WhatsApp army and 3 million new second-round voters, and look ahead to what a final Lula term will mean for the Brazilian left, the Amazon, and foreign policy. Then an even more extraordinary story from Serbia, where …

1 item in this evidence packet.

NEWSLETTER · 28 Oct 2026, 09:17

Lula’s Comeback Shows the Far Right Can Be Beaten

View this post on the web at https://josricardomartins.substack.com/p/lulas-comeback-shows-the-far-right

Brazil Has Chosen Democracy: Lula’s Victory Offers Hope Beyond Brazil Lula’s 4th term as president is more than just a personal victory. His defeat of Flávio Bolsonaro proves that the far right can be beaten, but the real challenge now is to convert that electoral support into real social progress, economic security and a renewed sense of democratic confidence. For Europe and the wider world, Brazil is a potent reminder that the right-wing wave is not necessarily the wave of the future. In one of the most pivotal elections of the year, Brazil has sent a message that resonates far beyond its borders. On October 25, President Lula da Silva of the left-wing Workers’ Party (PT) defeated far-right senator Flávio Bolsonaro in the presidential run-off, winning 51.8% of valid votes to 48.2%. At 80, Lula has been re-elected for a fourth, non-consecutive term. Having trailed after the first round, he turned the race around, something no candidate had achieved before in Brazil’s democratic history. The unexpected result also broke a string of right-wing and far-right victories across Latin America. Yet the significance of this victory cannot be measured in percentages alone. The contest was a battle over the political and social direction of Latin America’s largest nation. At stake was whether Brazil would continue to stand for democratic institutions, social protection, environmental responsibility and an independent foreign policy, or move towards a more authoritarian, socially exclusionary model aligned with the US’s political project. Lula’s win was a stinging defeat for the Bolsonaro family and their political allies, and a blow to the international far right more broadly. It also made it harder for US President Donald Trump to complete the construction of a conservative, Washington-led bloc across the Americas. The election showed that the region’s shift to the right is not necessarily a one-way street. In his victory speech, Lula sent an immediate message to Trump and Secretary of State Marco Rubio: “Brazil is no one’s backyard.” Trump, who had quietly backed Bolsonaro’s campaign, has made no public comment on the result, though his son Donald Trump Jr took aim at it in a post on X. Europe, by contrast, has warmly welcomed the outcome. European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen declared that “democracy and multilateralism have won in Brazil”, while other European leaders stressed the importance of continuing to work with Brazil on trade, climate and every other area of international co-operation. It was a clear acknowledgement that Brazil remains a vital partner at a time when the multilateral system, international co-operation and the rules-based order are under sustained pressure. The relief is palpable. Across Europe, democratic forces are confronting populist parties that play on cultural anxieties, hostility to immigration, distrust of institutions and a general sense of economic insecurity. Brazil has shown that such movements can be defeated at the polls, even when they command significant resources and a devoted base, operate in a deeply polarised society and enjoy US support. But the big lesson is not that Lula has beaten the far right once and for all. It is that the future remains open, and that organised citizens can shape it. The people returned to the streets and to the political debate One of the most remarkable features of Lula’s victory was the high level of popular mobilisation in the run-up to the second round. This was not simply a matter of the PT’s campaign machine, established political alliances or the president’s personal popularity. Indeed, Lula openly complained about his campaign’s (dis)organisation after the first round. As the prospect of a Flávio Bolsonaro victory became ever more tangible, a wider movement took shape, particularly among young people and progressive social groups, amplified by more intensive use of social media. Students, artists, activists and citizens of all kinds helped bring the campaign back into the public sphere. Social media became another battleground. Deputy André Janones played a central, pioneering role in mobilising online supporters through the “Exército de Lula” (Lula’s Army) WhatsApp network, which, according to Agência Brasil, had 10.8mn members. Its communication with voters was far more direct and informal in tone. This mobilisation mattered because the election was not just about Lula. Above all, it was a choice between two very different visions of Brazil. Many feared that a new Bolsonaro government would erode democratic safeguards, weaken the social safety net and further polarise Brazilian society along the lines that became clear in 2018, when Flávio’s father, convicted former president Jair Bolsonaro, was elected. The campaign thus became a defence of much more than one candidate, uniting people concerned about public healthcare, social assistance, workers’ rights, the environment, religious pluralism and, above all, the future of democracy itself. Brazilians who voted for Lula in the second round did not necessarily agree on every political issue. What they did agree on was that Brazil should not throw away the social and democratic gains it has painstakingly built over the years. Lula alluded to this in his post-election speech, thanking voters and stressing that no one can win an election without the people. It was also a clear warning for the future: a movement should not come together only when an election looms. The energy that brought Lula back from behind must be sustained through his next term, keeping the government on its toes and demanding real improvements in people’s lives. Brazilian democracy has survived authoritarianism, political crises and deep social divisions. Its strength has always lain in its citizens’ capacity to organise, protest and defend their rights. The historic mobilisation ahead of the run-off built on exactly that tradition. The election also exposed the dangers of the digital public sphere. Campaigns increasingly depend on platforms whose algorithms determine what voters see, share and believe. During the campaign, multiple allegations surfaced of unequal treatment and political bias by social media companies such as Meta, owner of Facebook, Instagram and WhatsApp. Leaks from Meta staff confirmed that the company was biased towards Bolsonaro, as did its own council and a UN expert panel. Meta officially blamed the shortcomings on technical problems. However, the disruptions were consistently one-sided, hampering the left’s efforts to amplify its messaging and leading to messages and accounts being blocked. These allegations raise a wider democratic question. Private technology companies, most of them US-based, should not be able to wield political power through opaque algorithms without transparency, accountability and appropriate safeguards. In his victory speech, Lula renewed his commitment to further regulating digital platforms. Democracy must deliver social justice Brazil’s choice was also a defence of a particular conception of the state, one in which public institutions have a duty to reduce poverty, protect the vulnerable and ensure basic services are available to all. Social policy was at the heart of the run-off. Welfare programmes such as Bolsa Família, Lula’s flagship cash-transfer scheme for the poor, are not simply handouts. They enable families to put food on the table, keep their children in school and tackle the structural inequalities that persist in Brazilian society. Universal public healthcare, real increases in the minimum wage, employment and access to education are equally part of this social contract. The World Bank has linked Brazil’s recent poverty reduction to a combination of real increases in the minimum wage, the expansion of Bolsa Família and better labour market conditions. These include a recently legislated maximum five-day working week, while free public transport for all at weekends (Saturday and Sunday) is due to be discussed soon. Such progress does not mean Brazil has solved inequality for good, but it does show that public policy can raise living standards when growth is combined with measures that effectively reach the poor. The right’s alternative, in fact, was not simply a pledge to scrap existing programmes. Later in the campaign, Flávio Bolsonaro and his allies went some way towards assuring voters that social benefits would remain. But that made the core argument of their campaign inconsistent: slash spending with Argentina’s Milei-style chainsaw while keeping Lula’s outlays. The numbers did not add up, and both market participants and his own voters questioned Bolsonaro’s credibility. As a result, the right’s message became far less convincing. Brazil has a long and painful history of slavery, racial inequality, regional disparities and concentrated wealth. Brazilians expect democracy to mean more than the right to vote. It must also mean the ability to live with dignity. Lula’s record provides grounds for defending this model, but there is still a long way to go. The environmental dimension is just as important. According to INPE, Brazil’s National Institute for Space Research, deforestation in the Amazon fell by 41.8% between August 2025 and July 2026 compared with the previous year, the lowest level in the 38-year monitoring series that began in 1988. Deforestation in the Cerrado also declined. This does not mean the country’s ecosystems are no longer under threat, but it does underline the value of enforcement and the need for continued policy and monitoring. Voters’ choice, then, was never simply an abstract one between left and right. It was about the kind of society Brazil wants to build. Sovereignty, multilateralism and a different international order Lula’s victory carries enormous geopolitical significance. Brazil is a major economy, set to climb from 10th to 8th in the global rankings in 2027, as well as a continental power, a leading agricultural producer and a significant diplomatic player. Its position has implications for Latin America, the Global South and the future of multilateralism. Sovereignty was the defining issue of the campaign when it came to Brazil’s international posture. Lula has been unequivocal that Brazil has the right to maintain relations with the US, China, the EU and all its other partners without accepting that Washington can dictate the terms of Brazilian foreign policy. His ironclad commitment to BRICS, south-south co-operation and a more multipolar world order is central to that stance. For Lula, however, strategic autonomy does not mean hostility towards the US. The leftist leader maintains good personal relations with Trump and instead blames Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whom he sometimes calls “the frustrated Latino”. Lula also prides himself on his strong relations with Xi Jinping, Vladimir Putin, Narendra Modi, Emmanuel Macron, and practically the rest of the world, though less so with Latin America’s hard-right governments. A former union negotiator, he is skilled at working with all kinds of partners. This matters all the more as competition intensifies over energy, critical minerals, advanced technology, trade and supply chains. In his victory speech, Lula said Brazil’s resources should underpin industrial development, scientific research and well-paid jobs at home, rather than leaving the country as just another exporter of raw materials and importer of processed goods. Sovereignty must have economic and social substance, he said. That means the capacity to generate value, develop technology and improve the lives of the Brazilian people. Lula’s victory also matters for the defence of multilateralism. Brazil has long championed diplomacy, supported international institutions and promoted co-operation across political divides. Its continued participation in BRICS and other international forums gives it the opportunity to act as a bridge between countries whose interests do not always align. However, Brazil’s natural leadership in South America has been damaged by the recent election of a large number of far-right governments that prefer direct alignment with Trump’s Latin America policy. For Europe, the stakes are particularly high. A democratic Brazil that supports open trade, notably the EU-Mercosur trade agreement, as well as climate action and multilateralism, is a very useful partner in an increasingly uncertain geopolitical landscape. The relationship should not be seen as a simple contest between Brussels, Washington and Beijing. It should rest on mutual interest, mutual respect for national sovereignty and a shared desire to work together on common problems. The sovereignty agenda has not gained the same traction in Europe as in Brazil, where voters’ choice was rooted in the country’s own history, inequalities, institutions and political struggles. Even so, the outcome offers a lesson: democratic states need not accept that international politics must be structured around authoritarian nationalism, perpetual confrontation and the subordination of the weak to the strong. Brazil now has the opportunity to show that sovereignty and international engagement are not mutually exclusive, and can in fact reinforce each other. The road ahead Brazil’s message is a positive one, but it by no means marks the end of the far-right threat. Flávio Bolsonaro took 48.2% of valid votes, and the right emerged from the legislative elections with considerable strength. Lula now faces a hostile conservative Congress and a very difficult road to getting his programme through. These realities demand political intelligence from Lula, not triumphalism. He must govern well beyond his own electorate, negotiate with the legislature without losing sight of his core principles, and show that a democratic government can in fact get things done. He will have to tackle soaring crime, the cost of living, employment and the state of public services while managing significant fiscal constraints. And he must do all this in a way that revitalises the progressive movement, so that its future does not depend entirely on one elderly man, particularly as this is his last election. The final point is the most important. Lula’s political career has been built on bringing broad coalitions together. For his fourth administration to be viable and democratic, it must involve the Brazilian people in decision-making, as he promised in his victory speech. The spontaneous mobilisation of young people in the run-off should be seen not as a mere electoral tool, but as a demand for a genuine role in shaping the country’s future. Europe, likewise, will defeat the far right only if it stays in touch with its people. Holding elections is not enough to make a democracy if governments do not serve the people. Geopolitical pressures close to home have led European leaders to relegate their own citizens to second or third place. We have even heard remarks such as: “I do not care what my voters think.” The far right cannot be kept at bay simply by telling people what a threat it poses. Brazil has not solved all these problems. Society remains deeply divided, the defeated candidate is questioning the result, institutions are under pressure, especially the Supreme Court, and the next government faces a very difficult set of challenges. The election has nonetheless shown something very significant: the populist conservative forces can be challenged, electoral momentum can be reversed, and popular mobilisation can make a real difference. That offers grounds for hope to democrats around the world. But defeating the far right at the ballot box is only the beginning. Lasting victory comes when democracy proves it can deliver a better life. Ricardo Martins is based in Utrecht, the Netherlands, and has a PhD in Sociology specialising in European politics, geopolitics and international relations.
